Second order McKean-Vlasov SDEs and kinetic Fokker-Planck-Kolmogorov equations Thanks: This work is partially supported by NNSFC grants of China (Nos. 12131019, 11731009), and the German Research Foundation (DFG) through the Collaborative Research Centre(CRC) 1283 “Taming uncertainty and profiting from randomness and low regularity in analysis, stochastics and their applications”.
Xicheng Zhang

Abstract
. In this paper we study second order stochastic differential equations with measurable and density-distribution dependent coefficients. Through establishing a maximum principle for kinetic Fokker-Planck-Kolmogorov equations with distribution-valued inhomogeneous term, we show the existence of weak solutions under mild assumptions. Moreover, by using the Hölder regularity estimate obtained recently in [ 16 ] , we also show the well-posedness of generalized martingale problems when diffusion coefficients only depend on the position variable (not necessarily continuous). Even in the non density-distribution dependent case, it seems that this is the first result about the well-posedness of SDEs with measurable diffusion coefficients. Keywords : Maximum principle, De-Giorgi’s iteration, Stochastic differential equation, Krylov’s estimate, kinetic Fokker-Planck-Kolmogorov equation. AMS 2010 Mathematics Subject Classification: 60H10, 35H10



1.
Introduction

In this paper we are concerned with the following second order stochastic differential equation
with density-distribution dependent coefficients (also called McKean-Vlasov SDE or simply DDSDE):

\displaystyle{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\dot{X}_{t}=b_{Z}(t,Z_{t}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}t+\sigma_{Z}(t,X_{t}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}W_{t},

(1.1)

where
Z_{t}:=(X_{t},\dot{X}_{t})
and
\dot{X}_{t}=:V_{t}
stands for the velocity,

(W_{t})_{t\geqslant 0}
is a
d
-dimensional standard Brownian motion defined on some stochastic basis
(\Omega,{\mathscr{F}},{\mathbf{P}};({\mathscr{F}}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0})
, and

\displaystyle b_{Z}(t,z):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}b(t,z,\rho_{Z_{t}}(z),z^{\prime})\mu_{Z_{t}}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z^{\prime}),

(1.2)

and

\displaystyle\sigma_{Z}(t,x):=\sqrt{2a_{Z}(t,x)},\ \ a_{Z}(t,x):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}a(t,x,\rho_{X_{t}}(x),z^{\prime})\mu_{Z_{t}}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z^{\prime}).

(1.3)

Here
\mu_{Z_{t}}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)={\mathbf{P}}\circ Z^{-1}_{t}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)=\rho_{Z_{t}}(z){\mathord{{\rm d}}}z
,
{\mathbf{P}}\circ X^{-1}_{t}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}x)=\rho_{X_{t}}(x){\mathord{{\rm d}}}x
, and

b(t,z,r,z^{\prime}):{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d}

and

a(t,x,r,z^{\prime}):{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}\to{\mathbb{M}}^{d}_{\rm sym}

are Borel measurable functions,
{\mathbb{M}}^{d}_{\rm sym}
is the set of all symmetric
d\times d
-matrices.
Throughout the paper, the density dependence means that
b_{Z},a_{Z}
point-wisely depend on the density of
Z
, while the distribution dependence means that

b_{Z},a_{Z}
non-locally depend on the distribution of
Z
in the whole space.
Below we suppose that
a
satisfies that for some
0<\kappa_{0}<\kappa_{1}
and all
t,x,r,z^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\kappa_{0}|\xi|^{2}\leqslant\xi\cdot a(t,x,r,z^{\prime})\xi\leqslant\kappa_{1}|\xi|^{2},\ \ \xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d},

(1.4)

where the dot stands for the inner product of two vectors.
Since the coefficients depend on the density and distribution of the solution itself, equation (
1.1
) is usually
regarded as a strong nonlinear SDE. Suppose that DDSDE (
1.1
) has a solution, whose meaning is given in Definition
1.1
below.
By Itô’s formula, it is easy to see that the density
\rho(t,z):=\rho_{Z_{t}}(z)
solves the following nonlinear kinetic Fokker-Planck-Kolmogorov equation (abbreviated as FPKE) in the distributional sense:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho=\partial_{v_{i}}\partial_{v_{j}}(\bar{a}_{ij}(\rho)\rho)-v\cdot\nabla_{x}\rho+\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}(\bar{b}(\rho)\rho),

(1.5)

where for
(t,x)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,

\bar{a}(\rho;t,x):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}a(t,x,\langle\rho\rangle(t,x),z^{\prime})\rho(t,z^{\prime})({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z^{\prime}),

and for
(t,z)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
,

\bar{b}(\rho;t,z):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}b(t,z,\rho(t,z),z^{\prime})\rho(t,z^{\prime})({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z^{\prime}).

Here and below,
\langle\rho\rangle(t,x):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\rho(t,x,v){\mathord{{\rm d}}}v
stands for the mass density, and we use the convention that repeated indices are summed automatically.

An important prototype of PDE (
1.5
) is the following Landau equation or its variant form (see
[
21
,
22
,
1
,
30
]
):

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\rho=\partial_{v_{i}}\left\{\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}a_{ij}(v-v^{\prime})\left[\rho(v^{\prime})\partial_{v_{j}}\rho(v)-\rho(v)\partial_{v^{\prime}_{j}}\rho(v^{\prime})\right]{\mathord{{\rm d}}}v^{\prime}\right\},

(1.6)

where
\rho=\rho(t,x,v)
and for
v=(v_{1},\cdots,v_{d})
,

a_{ij}(v):=c_{d,\gamma}[\delta_{ij}-v_{i}v_{j}/|v|^{2}]/|v|^{\gamma+d},\ \ \gamma\in[-d,+\infty),\ c_{d,\gamma}>0,

which models the behavior of a dilute plasma interacting through binary collisions (see
[
28
]
).
While nonlinear SDE (
1.1
) with distribution dependent coefficients
was firstly proposed by McKean
[
24
]
to give a probabilistic explanation for nonlinear Vlasov equations.
Nowadays, McKean-Vlasov SDEs have beed widely used in the study of interacting particle systems and mean-field games
(cf.
[
32
,
6
]
and references therein).
Recently, there are many works to study the well-posedness of first order nondegenerate DDSDEs with rough coefficients.
When
b
and
a
does not depend on the density variable
r
and of at most linear growth, by using the classical Krylov estimates,
Mishura and Veretennikov
[
25
]
showed the existence of weak solutions for first order DDSDEs.
The uniqueness is also proved when
a
does not depend on
z^{\prime}
and is Lipschitz continuous in
x
.
Later, in
[
27
]
their results were extended to the case that drift
b
is in
L^{p}
-spaces. More results and references about McKean-Vlasov SDEs
are summarized in the paper of
[
14
]
(see also the references in
[
27
]
). For nonlinear SDEs with density dependent coefficients (also called McKean-Vlasov SDEs of Nemytskii-type),
it was firstly studied in
[
3
]
to give a probabilistic representation for the solution of nonlinear FPKEs.
In
[
4
]
, for a large class of time independent coefficients
b
,
a
, Barbu and Röckner
obtained the existence of weak solutions for first order density dependent SDEs. The strategy in
[
3
]
and
[
4
]

is to solve the associated nonlinear FPKE and then by the well- known superposition principle
to establish the existence of a weak solution to the first order DDSDE.
In
[
11
]
, Hao, Röckner and the present author give a purely probabilistic
proof for the existence of weak solutions for the first order density dependent SDE by Euler’s scheme.
Our current degenerate kinetic settings are more general and is not studied in the literature yet.

As usual, to study DDSDE (
1.1
), we must have better understanding for the associated kinetic FPKEs. In particular, we need to develop the hypoelliptic regularity estimates for kinetic FPKEs with
rough coefficients. In
[
26
]
, Pascucci and Polidoro obtained the upper bound estimate of weak solutions for a class of ultraparabolic equations of divergence form via Moser’s method,
especially, including the following kinetic equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u=\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}(a\cdot\nabla_{v}u)+v\cdot\nabla_{x}u.

(1.7)

In
[
34
,
35
]
, Wang and Zhang showed the Hölder regularity of weak solutions to the above equation.
In
[
16
]
, Golse, Imbert, Mouhot and Vasseur showed the Harnack inequality for (
1.7
) with bounded first order and inhomogeneous terms.
As an application, they also showed the conditional Hölder regularity for the Landau equation (
1.6
). More recently, weak Harnack inequality and some quantitative estimates
for kinetic FPKEs were obtained in
[
17
,
18
]
. More historical backgrounds about Harnack inequalities and kinetic equations are referred to
[
17
, Section 1.2]
.

1.1.
Main results

Let
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
be the space of all probability measures over
{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
.
We first introduce the following notion of weak solutions to DDSDE (
1.1
).

Definition 1.1
.

Let
\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
and
{\mathfrak{F}}:=(\Omega,{\mathscr{F}},{\mathbf{P}};({\mathscr{F}}_{t})_{t\geqslant 0})
be a stochastic basis,
Z_{t}=(X_{t},V_{t})
and
W_{t}

be
{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
and
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
-valued
{\mathscr{F}}_{t}
-adapted processes, respectively.
We call
({\mathfrak{F}},Z,W)
a weak solution of DDSDE (
1.1
) with initial distribution
\nu
if

(i)

{\mathbf{P}}\circ Z^{-1}_{0}=\nu
, and for Lebesgue almost all
t\geqslant 0
,

{\mathbf{P}}\circ Z^{-1}_{t}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)=\rho_{Z_{t}}(z){\mathord{{\rm d}}}z,\ \ {\mathbf{P}}\circ X^{-1}_{t}({\mathord{{\rm d}}}x)=\rho_{X_{t}}(x){\mathord{{\rm d}}}x.

(ii)

W
is a standard
d
-dimensional
{\mathscr{F}}_{t}
-Brownian motion.

(iii)

For all
t\geqslant 0
, it holds that
X_{t}=X_{0}+\int^{t}_{0}V_{s}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s
and

V_{t}=V_{0}+\int^{t}_{0}b_{Z}(s,Z_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+\int^{t}_{0}\sigma_{Z}(s,X_{s}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}W_{s},\ {\mathbf{P}}-a.s.,

where
b_{Z}
and
\sigma_{Z}
are defined by (
1.2
) and (
1.3
), respectively.

Before stating our main results, we make the following assumptions about
b
and
a
:

(H
1
)

For any
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
and bounded domain
Q\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\left\|\sup_{r,r^{\prime}\leqslant m,|r-r^{\prime}|\leqslant h}|b(\cdot,\cdot,r,\cdot)-b(\cdot,\cdot,r^{\prime},\cdot)|\right\|_{L^{1}(Q)}=0,

(1.8)

and for all
(t,z,r,z^{\prime})\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
,

\displaystyle|b(t,z,r,z^{\prime})|\leqslant h(t,z-z^{\prime})\mbox{ with }\ |\mspace{-3mu}|\mspace{-3mu}|h|\mspace{-3mu}|\mspace{-3mu}|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{q_{1}}_{t}(\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}_{z})}\leqslant\kappa_{2},

(1.9)

where
q_{1}\in(2,4)
and
{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}\in(2,\infty)^{2d}
satisfy
{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}+\frac{2}{q_{1}}<1
,
the localized norm

|\mspace{-3mu}|\mspace{-3mu}|\cdot|\mspace{-3mu}|\mspace{-3mu}|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{q_{1}}_{t}(\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}_{z})}
is defined by (
5.2
) below.
Here
{\boldsymbol{a}}=(3,\cdots,3,1,\cdots,1)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
(see (
3.12
) below).

(H
2
)

In addition to ellipticity assumption (
1.4
), we assume that for each
(t,x,z)\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
,
[0,\infty)\ni r\mapsto a(t,x,r,z)\in{\mathbb{M}}^{d}_{\rm sym}

is continuous.

Remark 1.2
.

The continuity of
[0,\infty)\ni r\mapsto a(t,x,r,z)\in{\mathbb{M}}^{d}_{\rm sym}
together with the boundedness of
a
implies that
for any bounded
Q\subset{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{h\to 0}\left\|\sup_{r,r^{\prime}\leqslant m,|r-r^{\prime}|\leqslant h}|a(\cdot,\cdot,r,\cdot)-a(\cdot,\cdot,r^{\prime},\cdot)|\right\|_{L^{1}(Q)}=0.

(1.10)

Indeed, it follows by the dominated convergence theorem and for each
(t,x,z^{\prime})
,

\lim_{h\to 0}\sup_{r,r^{\prime}\leqslant m,|r-r^{\prime}|\leqslant h}|a(t,x,r,z^{\prime})-a(t,x,r^{\prime},z^{\prime})|=0.

First of all, we have the following existence result of weak solutions.

Theorem 1.3
.

Under
(H
1
)
and
(H
2
)
, for any
\nu\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
,
there exists at least one weak solution to SDE (
1.1
) with initial distribution
\nu
in the sense of Definition
1.1
.
In particular, the probability distributional density
\rho_{t}
of
Z_{t}
solves (
1.5
) with initial value being the probability measure
\nu
.
Moreover,
\rho
enjoys the following regularity: for any
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
(q,{\boldsymbol{p}})\in(1,\infty)^{1+2d}
satisfying

\tfrac{2}{q}<1+\alpha,\ \ \ \tfrac{2}{q}+{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}-{\mathbf{1}})>2\alpha,

it holds that

\displaystyle\|\rho{\mathbf{1}}_{[0,T]}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{q}_{t}({\mathbf{B}}^{\alpha}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}})}<\infty,\ T>0,

(1.11)

where
{\mathbf{B}}^{\alpha}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}
is the anisotropic Besov space in Definition
2.1
.

Remark 1.4
.

(i) Suppose that
b
and
a
do not depend on the density variable
r
, then we can drop the assumption
q_{1}\in(2,4)
in
(H
1
)

but require
\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}+\frac{{\mathbf{1}}}{q_{1}}<\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{2}}}
(see Remark
6.12
below).

(ii) We explain the use of
multi-integrability index
{\boldsymbol{p}}\in(2,\infty)^{2d}
(see (
2.1
) below). Consider the following second order SDE in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
:

\left\{\begin{aligned} {\mathord{{\rm d}}}\dot{X}^{1}_{t}=b_{1}(X^{1}_{t}-X^{2}_{t}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}t+{\mathord{{\rm d}}}W^{1}_{t},\\
{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\dot{X}^{2}_{t}=b_{2}(X^{1}_{t}-X^{2}_{t}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}t+{\mathord{{\rm d}}}W^{2}_{t},\end{aligned}\right.

where
b_{1},b_{2}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
are two measurable functions.
If we write

X_{t}:=(X^{1}_{t},X^{2}_{t}),\ \ Z_{t}:=(X_{t},\dot{X}_{t}),\ \ W_{t}:=(W^{1}_{t},W^{2}_{t})

and

b(x_{1},x_{2},v_{1},v_{2}):=(b_{1},b_{2})(x_{1}-x_{2}):{\mathbb{R}}^{4}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2},

then the above SDE can be written in the form of (
1.1
).
Suppose now that
b_{1},b_{2}\in\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{p}
for some
p>3
. Then by the definition (
5.1
) below,
it is easy to see that (
1.9
) holds for
{\boldsymbol{p}}=(p,p^{\prime},p^{\prime},p^{\prime})
, where
p^{\prime}
is chosen large enough so that

{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=\frac{3}{p}+\frac{3}{p^{\prime}}+\frac{2}{p^{\prime}}<1
. However, if we do not use the above multi-index
{\boldsymbol{p}}
, then we have to require
p>8
.
To the author’s knowledge, Ling and Xie
[
23
]
firstly used the mixed-
L^{\boldsymbol{p}}
norm for studying singular first order SDEs. We emphasize that
such a feature naturally appears in the study of interacting particle systems with singular interaction force, especially, propagation of chaos (cf.
[
32
]
).
We shall study this in a future work.

Although we have shown the existence of weak solutions under very mild assumptions in the above theorem,
the uniqueness is a more subtle problem. In fact, in the non-distribution dependent case, that is,

a
and
b
does not depend on
(r,z^{\prime})
, when
a
is uniformly continuous and
b
is Hölder continuous or in some
L^{p}
-spaces,
the uniqueness of weak solutions or strong solutions for second order SDEs was obtained in
[
7
,
36
,
37
,
8
]
via Zvonkin’s transformation.
Recently, when
a
is the identity matrix and
b=b_{1}+b_{2}
, where
b_{1}(t,z)
is a distribution of
z
and
b_{2}(t,z,z^{\prime})
is bounded measurable,
the well-posedness of generalized martingale problem associated with SDE (
1.1
) was obtained in
[
13
]
,
where the generalized martingale problem is taken in the sense of Either and Kurtz
[
9
, Chapter 4]
. It should be noted that the notion of generalized martingale solutions
strongly depends on the solvability of the associated backward Kolmogorov equations (see Definition
7.1
below for a precise description).
To show the uniqueness, we now suppose that

(H
3
)

a=a(t,x)
is independent of
(r,z^{\prime})
and satisfies (
1.4
), and
b
is bounded measurable and satisfies that for
some
C>0
and all
(t,z,z^{\prime})\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
and
r,r^{\prime}\geqslant 0
,

|b(t,z,r,z^{\prime})-b(t,z,r^{\prime},z^{\prime})|\leqslant C|r-r^{\prime}|.

We have the following well-posedness result of generalized martingale problems.

Theorem 1.5
.

Under
(H
3
)
, for any
s\geqslant 0
and
\nu({\mathord{{\rm d}}}z)=\rho_{0}(z){\mathord{{\rm d}}}z
, where
\rho_{0}\in C^{1}_{b}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
,
there is a unique generalized martingale solution
{\mathbb{P}}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{a,b}_{s,\nu}
with initial distribution
\nu
at time
s

in the sense of Definition
7.1
below. Moreover, its density
\rho_{t}(z)
enjoys the regularity (
1.11
) and solves the following nonlinear kinetic FPKE in the distributional sense:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\rho_{t}=\partial_{v_{i}}\partial_{v_{j}}(a_{ij}\rho_{t})-v\cdot\nabla_{x}\rho_{t}+\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}(b_{\rho}\rho_{t}),\ t\geqslant s,

(1.12)

where
b_{\rho}(t,z):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}b(t,z,\rho_{t}(z),z^{\prime})\rho_{t}(z^{\prime}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}z^{\prime}
.

Remark 1.6
.

(i) If
b
does not depend on the density variable
r
, then we can drop the regularity assumption on the initial distribution
\nu
(see Remark
7.3
below).
In particular, suppose that
a=a(x)
satisfies (
1.4
) and
b=b(z)
is bounded measurable,
then for each starting point
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
, the following second order SDE admits a unique generalized martingale solution

{\mathbb{P}}_{z}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{a,b}_{0,\delta_{z}}
in the sense of Definition
7.1
:

\displaystyle{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\dot{X}_{t}=b(Z_{t}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}t+\sqrt{a}(X_{t}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}W_{t},\ \ Z_{0}=z.

(1.13)

Moreover, let
b_{\varepsilon}
and
a_{\varepsilon}
be the smooth approximation of
b
and
a
. Consider the following SDE:

{\mathord{{\rm d}}}\dot{X}^{\varepsilon}_{t}=b_{\varepsilon}(Z^{\varepsilon}_{t}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}t+\sqrt{a_{\varepsilon}}(X^{\varepsilon}_{t}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}W_{t},\ \ Z^{\varepsilon}_{0}=z.

By the uniqueness, for each
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
, the law of
Z^{\varepsilon}
weakly converges to
{\mathbb{P}}_{z}
(not necessarily subtracting a subsequence).
In particular, the family of probability measures
({\mathbb{P}}_{z})_{z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}

on the space of continuous functions forms a family of strong Markov processes (see
[
9
, p.184, Theorem 4.4.2]
). In fact, from the proof below, one can see that for each
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}
,

{\mathbb{P}}_{z}
is also a classical martingale solution in the sense of Definition
6.1
. However,
it is not known whether
any
classical martingale solution priorily is a generalized martingale solution.
This seems to be an open problem.

(ii) Consider the following first order SDE in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
,

{\mathord{{\rm d}}}X_{t}=b(X_{t}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}t+\sqrt{a}(X_{t}){\mathord{{\rm d}}}W_{t},\ \ X_{0}=x.

When
b
is bounded measurable and
a
satisfies the ellipticity condition (
1.4
),
it is well known that there is at least one weak solution to the above SDE (see
[
19
]
or
[
31
, Ex. 7.3.2]
). Moreover, for
d=1,2
, the weak uniqueness also holds (see
[
31
, Ex. 7.3.3, 7.3.4]
).
However, for
d\geqslant 3
, to get the weak uniqueness, one usually needs to assume
a
being continuous (see
[
31
, Theorem 7.2.1]
). For the strong uniqueness, the well-known best condition for
a
seems to be
a\in{\mathbb{W}}^{2,p}
, the second order Sobolev space, where
p>d
(see
[
38
]
).
It is interesting that for second order SDEs with discontinuous diffusion coefficients, we have the well-posedness of generalized martingale problems.

The crux point in our proof is to establish a maximum principle for the following kinetic FPKE:

\partial_{t}u=\mathrm{tr}(a\cdot\nabla^{2}_{v}u)+v\cdot\nabla_{x}u+b_{1}\cdot\nabla_{v}u+\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}(b_{2}u)+f,\ \ u(0)=\varphi,

where an important observation is that since the diffusion-matrix
a
does not depend on the velocity variable
v
, the principal second order term can be written in divergence form:

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}(a\cdot\nabla^{2}_{v}u)=\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}(a\cdot\nabla_{v}u).

(1.14)

Thus one can invoke the classical De-Giorgi method to show the apriori
L^{\infty}
-estimates.
Compared with the recent works
[
16
,
17
,
18
]
, the novelty of our boundedness estimates is that
b
can be in some
{\mathbb{L}}^{q_{1}}_{t}({\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}_{z})
-space, where

(q_{1},{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1})\in(2,\infty)^{1+2d}
satisfies
{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}+\frac{2}{q_{1}}<1
,
and
f
is in some
{\mathbb{L}}^{q_{0}}_{t}({\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0}}_{z})
-space, where

(q_{0},{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0})\in(1,\infty)^{1+2d}
satisfies
{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{0}}+\frac{2}{q_{0}}<2
.
More importantly,
f
is also allowed to be a distribution in some Besov spaces with negative differentiability index, which plays a crucial role to show
the existence of weak solutions in Theorem
1.3
. That is, the distribution valued
f
provides us the regularity of the density so that one can show the strong convergence of the densities of approximating equations (see Lemma
6.9
below).

1.2.
Plan of the paper

This paper is organized as follows.
In Section 2, we prepare some preliminary results for later use. More concretely,
in Subsection 2.1, we introduce necessary anisotropic Besov spaces and some basic properties related to the Besov spaces.
It is noted that our
L^{p}
-spaces have different integrability indices along different components as explained in Remark
1.4
above.
In Subsection 2.2 we provide an abstract criterion for the local bound of
a function in De-Giorgi’s class. Compared with the classical notion of De-Giorgi’s class
[
20
]
,
our definition does not depend on any structure of PDEs and is only in the scope of
L^{p}
-spaces.

In Section 3, we recall some well-known properties about weak (sub)-solutions.
In Subsection 3.2, we show how to improve the regularity in Besov spaces via Duhamel’s formula for kinetic equations, which is essentially proved in
[
12
,
39
]
.
Moreover, we also show the existence and uniqueness of weak solutions for the Cauchy problem of a linear kinetic FPKE (see Theorem
3.9
), which seems to be new.

Section 4 is devoted to showing the local boundedness of weak solutions via De-Giorgi’s method. Two cases are considered. In the first case that the nonhomogeneous
f
is an
L^{p}
-function,
we use the comparison method as in
[
16
]
to improve the regularity of weak sub-solutions. In this case, we have best integrability conditions on drift
b
and
inhomogeneous
f
as stated above. In the second case that the nonhomogeneous
f
is allowed to be a distribution, we use the simple fact that for a weak solution
u
,

(u^{+})^{2}
is still a weak solution with
2fu^{+}-2\langle a\cdot\nabla_{v}u^{+},\nabla_{v}u^{+}\rangle
in place of
f
(see Lemma
3.5
below). In this case, the
L^{\infty}
-estimate of weak solutions
in Theorem
4.5
shall provide extra Besov regularity for the density of second order SDEs so that we can treat the density dependent SDEs (see Corollary
6.7
).

In Section 5, we show the well-posedness and stability of weak solutions for PDE (
3.1
).
In particular, we obtain the global bound estimate and stability of weak solutions, where the key point is to use
the localization norm
|\mspace{-3mu}|\mspace{-3mu}|\cdot|\mspace{-3mu}|\mspace{-3mu}|_{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{q}_{t}(\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{z})}
introduced in
[
39
]
. For the stability, we use the Hölder regularity estimate established in

[
16
]
(see also
[
34
,
17
,
18
]
).

In Section 6, we prove the existence of weak solutions. As usual, in terms of the equivalence between weak solutions and martingale problems,
we show the existence of classical martingale solutions associated with DDSDE (
1.1
) via mollifying the coefficients. Since our diffusion coefficient
does not depend on velocity variable, the Kolmogorov equation associated SDEs (
1.1
) can be written in the divergence form as explained in (
1.14
).
Thus one can utilize the maximum principle obtained in Section 5 to show the crucial Krylov estimate for approximating equation.
Then by Aubin-Lions’ lemma, one can find a strong convergence subsequence of the densities.

In Section 7, we show the well-posedness of generalized martingale problems. As said above,
such a notion strongly depends on the solvability of the associated Kolmogorov equations. When the solution of the associated Kolmogorov equations has enough regularity, saying
C^{2}
-smooth,
the classical martingale solution must be a generalized martingale solution. In general, these two notions are not comparable. The advantage of using
the notion of generalized martingale solutions is that
the uniqueness is an easy consequence of the unique solvability of the associated Kolmogorov equation. The disadvantage is that it has few flexibility
and Itô’s formula is not applicable so that it
is not direct to see that the density of the solution solves the Fokker-Planck-Kolmogorov equation.

We conclude this introduction by introducing the following convention:
Throughout this paper, we use
C
with or without subscripts to denote an unimportant constant, whose value
may change in different occasions. We also use
:=
as a way of definition and
a^{+}:=0\vee a
. By
A\lesssim_{C}B
and
A\asymp_{C}B

or simply
A\lesssim B
and
A\asymp B
, we mean that for some unimportant constant
C\geqslant 1
,

A\leqslant CB,\ \ C^{-1}B\leqslant A\leqslant CB.

2.
Preliminary

2.1.
Anisotropic Besov spaces

Fix
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
. For multi-index
{\boldsymbol{p}}=(p_{1},\cdots,p_{N})\in(0,\infty]^{N}
, we define

\displaystyle\|f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}}:=\Bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}}\cdots\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}}|f(z_{1},\cdots,z_{N})|^{p_{N}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z_{N}\right)^{\frac{p_{N-1}}{p_{N}}}\cdots{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z_{2}\Bigg)^{\frac{p_{1}}{p_{2}}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}z_{1}\Bigg)^{\frac{1}{p_{1}}}.

(2.1)

When
{\boldsymbol{p}}=(p,\cdots,p)\in(0,\infty]^{N}
, we shall simply write

{\mathbb{L}}^{p}={\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}.

Note that if
{\boldsymbol{p}}\in[1,\infty]^{N}
, then
\|\cdot\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{z}}
satisfies the triangle inequality and is a norm,
and for any permutation
{\boldsymbol{p}}^{\prime}
of
{\boldsymbol{p}}
,

\|f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}}\not=\|f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}^{\prime}}}.

For multi-indices
{\boldsymbol{p}},{\boldsymbol{q}}\in(0,\infty]^{N}
, we shall use the following notations:

\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}:=\Big(\frac{1}{p_{1}},\cdots,\frac{1}{p_{N}}\Big),\ \ \ {\boldsymbol{p}}\cdot{\boldsymbol{q}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{N}p_{i}q_{i},

and

{\boldsymbol{p}}>{\boldsymbol{q}}\ ({\boldsymbol{p}}\geqslant{\boldsymbol{q}};\ {\boldsymbol{p}}={\boldsymbol{q}})\Leftrightarrow p_{i}>q_{i}\ (p_{i}\geqslant q_{i};\ p_{i}=q_{i}),\ \ i=1,\cdots,N.

Moreover, we use bold number to denote constant vector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
:

\boldsymbol{1}=(1,\cdots,1),\ \ \boldsymbol{2}=(2,\cdots,2).

With a little confusion, for a set
Q\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
, we also use
{\mathbf{1}}_{Q}
to denote the indicator function of
Q
.
For any multi-indices
{\boldsymbol{p}},{\boldsymbol{q}},{\boldsymbol{r}}\in(0,\infty]^{N}
with
\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}+\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{r}}}=\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}
, the following Hölder’s inequality holds

\|fg\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{q}}}\leqslant\|f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}}\|g\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{r}}}.

For any multi-indices
{\boldsymbol{p}},{\boldsymbol{q}},{\boldsymbol{r}}\in[1,\infty]^{N}
with
\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}+\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{r}}}=\boldsymbol{1}+\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}
, the following Young’s inequality holds

\|f*g\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{q}}}\leqslant\|f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}}\|g\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{r}}}.

These two inequalities are easy consequences of the well-known Hölder and Young’s inequalities for
{\boldsymbol{p}}=(p,\cdots,p)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{a}}=(a_{1},\cdots,a_{N})\in[1,\infty)^{N}
be a multi-scaling parameter.
For
z,z^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
, we introduce the following anisotropic distance in
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}

|z-z^{\prime}|_{\boldsymbol{a}}:=\sum_{i=1}^{N}|z_{i}-z^{\prime}_{i}|^{1/a_{i}}.

For
r>0
and
z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
, we also introduce the ball with respect to the above distance

B^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{r}(z):=\{z^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}:|z^{\prime}-z|_{\boldsymbol{a}}\leqslant r\},\ \ B^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{r}:=B^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{r}(0).

Let
\phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0}
be a symmetric
C^{\infty}
-function on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
with

\phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0}(\xi)=1\ \mathrm{for}\ \xi\in B^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{1}\ \mathrm{and}\ \phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0}(\xi)=0\ \mathrm{for}\ \xi\notin B^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{2}.

For
j\in\mathbb{N}
, we define

\phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}(\xi):=\phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0}(2^{-j{\boldsymbol{a}}}\xi)-\phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{0}(2^{-(j-1){\boldsymbol{a}}}\xi),

where for
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\xi=(\xi_{1},\cdots,\xi_{N})
,

2^{s{\boldsymbol{a}}}\xi=(2^{sa_{1}}\xi_{1},\cdots,2^{sa_{N}}\xi_{N}).

For an
L^{1}
-integrable function
f
, let
\hat{f}
be the Fourier transform of
f
defined by

\hat{f}(\xi):=(2\pi)^{-N/2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{N}}\mathrm{e}^{-{\rm i}\xi\cdot z}f(z){\mathord{{\rm d}}}z,\quad\xi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N},

and
\check{f}
the Fourier inverse transform of
f
defined by

\check{f}(z):=(2\pi)^{-N/2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{N}}\mathrm{e}^{{\rm i}\xi\cdot z}f(\xi){\mathord{{\rm d}}}\xi,\quad z\in{\mathbb{R}}^{N}.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be the space of all Schwartz functions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
and
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
the dual space of
{\mathcal{S}}
, called the tempered distribution space.
For given
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the block operator
\mathcal{R}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}
is defined on
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f(z):=(\phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}\hat{f})\check{\ }(z)=\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}*f(z),

(2.2)

where the convolution is understood in the distributional sense and by scaling,

\displaystyle\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}(z)=2^{(j-1)|{\boldsymbol{a}}|}\check{\phi}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{1}(2^{(j-1){\boldsymbol{a}}}z).

(2.3)

Here and after, we denote

|{\boldsymbol{a}}|:=a_{1}+\cdots+a_{N}={\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot{\mathbf{1}}.

For
j\geqslant 0
, by definition it is easy to see that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}={\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j},\ \mbox{ where }\ \widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}:={\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j-1}+{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}+{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j+1}\mbox{ with }{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{-1}\equiv 0,

(2.4)

and by the symmetry of
\phi^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}
,

\langle{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f,g\rangle=\langle f,{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}g\rangle,\ \ f\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime},\ g\in{\mathcal{S}}.

The cut-off low frequency operator
S_{k}
is defined by

\displaystyle S_{k}f:=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\to f,\ \ k\to\infty.

(2.5)

For
f,g\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
, define

\displaystyle f\prec g:=\sum_{k\geqslant 0}S_{k-1}f{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{k}g,\ \ f\circ g:=\sum_{|i-j|\leqslant 1}{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{i}f{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}g.

(2.6)

The Bony decomposition of
fg
is formally given by (cf.
[
2
,
5
]
)

\displaystyle fg=f\prec g+f\circ g+g\prec f.

(2.7)

Now we introduce the following anisotropic Besov spaces (cf.
[
33
, Chapter 5]
).

Definition 2.1
.

(Anisotropic Besov space)
For
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
{\boldsymbol{p}}\in[1,\infty]^{N}
, we define

\mathbf{B}^{s}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}:=\left\{f\in{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}:\|f\|_{\mathbf{B}^{s}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}:=\sup_{j\geqslant 0}\big(2^{sj}\|\mathcal{R}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}}\big)<\infty\right\}.

Remark 2.2
.

For
s\in(0,1)
and
{\boldsymbol{p}}\in[1,\infty]^{N}
, it is well known that
\mathbf{B}^{s}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}
has the following characterization (see
[
33
]
or
[
13
, Theorem 2.7]
):

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathbf{B}^{s}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\asymp\|f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}+\sup_{h}\|f(\cdot+h)-f(\cdot)\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}/|h|^{s}_{\boldsymbol{a}}.

(2.8)

Although the proof in
[
13
]
is for
{\boldsymbol{p}}=(p,\cdots,p)
, it also works for general
{\boldsymbol{p}}\in[1,\infty]^{N}
.

The following inequality of Bernstein’s type is quite useful (cf.
[
2
]
).

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{p}},{\boldsymbol{q}}\in[1,\infty]^{N}
with
{\boldsymbol{p}}\leqslant{\boldsymbol{q}}
.
For any
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and
i=1,\cdots,N
,
there is a constant
C=C({\boldsymbol{p}},{\boldsymbol{q}},{\boldsymbol{a}},k,i)>0
such that for all
j\geqslant 0
,

\displaystyle\|\partial_{z_{i}}^{k}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{\boldsymbol{a}}f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{q}}}\lesssim_{C}2^{j(a_{i}k+{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}-\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}))}\|{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{\boldsymbol{a}}f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}},

(2.9)

where
\partial_{z_{i}}^{k}
denotes the
k
-order partial derivative with respect to
z_{i}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{\boldsymbol{a}}f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}}\lesssim_{C}\|f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}},\ j\geqslant 0.

(2.10)

Proof.

We only prove (
2.9
) for
j\geqslant 1
. Let
{\boldsymbol{r}}\in[1,\infty]^{N}
be defined by

\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}+\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{r}}}={\mathbf{1}}+\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}.

By (
2.4
), (
2.2
) and Young’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\|\partial_{z_{i}}^{k}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{\boldsymbol{a}}f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{q}}}=\|\partial_{z_{i}}^{k}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{\boldsymbol{a}}{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{\boldsymbol{a}}f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{q}}}=\|(\partial_{z_{i}}^{k}\check{\widetilde{\phi_{j}^{\boldsymbol{a}}}})*{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{\boldsymbol{a}}f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{q}}}\lesssim\|\partial_{z_{i}}^{k}\check{\widetilde{\phi_{j}^{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{r}}}\|{\mathcal{R}}_{j}^{\boldsymbol{a}}f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}},

where

\widetilde{\phi_{j}^{\boldsymbol{a}}}:=\phi_{j-1}^{\boldsymbol{a}}+\phi_{j}^{\boldsymbol{a}}+\phi_{j+1}^{\boldsymbol{a}}.

By (
2.3
) and the change of variables, we have

\displaystyle\|\partial_{z_{i}}^{k}\check{\widetilde{\phi_{j}^{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{r}}}=2^{(j-1)a_{i}k}2^{(j-1){\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot({\mathbf{1}}-\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{r}}})}\|\partial_{z_{i}}^{k}\check{\widetilde{\phi_{1}^{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{r}}}.

Thus we get (
2.9
).
∎

We have the following interpolation inequality of Gagliado-Nirenberge’s type.

Lemma 2.4
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{p}},{\boldsymbol{q}},{\boldsymbol{r}}\in[1,\infty]^{N}
and
s,s_{0},s_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
\theta\in[0,1]
. Suppose that

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}\leqslant\tfrac{1-\theta}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}+\tfrac{\theta}{{\boldsymbol{r}}},\ \ s-{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=(1-\theta)\big(s_{0}-{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}\big)+\theta\big(s_{1}-{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{r}}}\big).

(2.11)

Then there is a constant
C=C({\boldsymbol{p}},{\boldsymbol{q}},{\boldsymbol{r}},s,s_{0},s_{1},\theta)>0
such that

\displaystyle\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\lesssim_{C}\|f\|^{1-\theta}_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s_{0}}_{{\boldsymbol{q}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\|f\|^{\theta}_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s_{1}}_{{\boldsymbol{r}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}.

(2.12)

Proof.

For
\theta=0,1
, (
2.12
) is direct by (
2.9
). Below we assume
\theta\in(0,1)
.
Let
{\boldsymbol{r}}\leqslant{\boldsymbol{w}}\in[1,\infty]^{N}
and
s_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}
be defined by

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}=\tfrac{1-\theta}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}+\tfrac{\theta}{{\boldsymbol{w}}},\ \ s_{0}(1-\theta)+s_{2}\theta=s.

(2.13)

By Hölder’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}}

\displaystyle=\|({\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f)^{1-\theta}({\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f)^{\theta}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}}\leqslant\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|^{1-\theta}_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|^{\theta}_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{w}}}}.

Hence,

\displaystyle 2^{js}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\Big(2^{js_{0}}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}\Big)^{1-\theta}\Big(2^{js_{2}}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{w}}}}\Big)^{\theta}.

(2.14)

Note that by (
2.11
) and (
2.13
),

s_{1}=s_{2}+{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{r}}}-\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{w}}}),

and by (
2.9
),

\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{w}}}}\lesssim 2^{j{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{r}}}-\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{w}}})}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{r}}}},\ j\geqslant 0.

Substituting it into (
2.14
), we obtain

2^{js}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}}\lesssim\Big(2^{js_{0}}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}\Big)^{1-\theta}\Big(2^{js_{1}}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}\Big)^{\theta}\leqslant\|f\|^{1-\theta}_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s_{0}}_{{\boldsymbol{q}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\|f\|^{\theta}_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s_{1}}_{{\boldsymbol{q}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}.

The proof is complete.
∎

We also need the following two simple results. Since they are crucial for treating distribution-valued inhomogeneous
f
,
we provide detailed proofs here for readers’ convenience.

Lemma 2.5
.

For any
s^{\prime}>s>0
and
{\boldsymbol{p}},{\boldsymbol{q}}\in[1,\infty]^{N}
with
\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}+\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}={\mathbf{1}}
, there is a constant
C=C({\boldsymbol{p}},{\boldsymbol{q}},s,s^{\prime},N)>0

such that for all
f\in{\mathbf{B}}^{-s}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}
and
g\in{\mathbf{B}}^{s^{\prime}}_{{\boldsymbol{q}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}
,

|\langle\!\!\!\langle f,g\rangle\!\!\!\rangle|+\|fg\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{-s}_{1;{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\lesssim_{C}\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{-s}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\|g\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s^{\prime}}_{{\boldsymbol{q}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}},

where
\langle\!\!\!\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle\!\!\!\rangle
stands for the dual pair between
{\mathcal{S}}^{\prime}
and
{\mathcal{S}}
.

Proof.

By (
2.5
), (
2.4
) and Hölder’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\langle\!\!\!\langle f,g\rangle\!\!\!\rangle=\sum_{j\geqslant 0}\langle\!\!\!\langle{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f,\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}g\rangle\!\!\!\rangle\leqslant\sum_{j\geqslant 0}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}g\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{q}}}\leqslant\sum_{j\geqslant 0}2^{(s-s^{\prime})j}\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{-s}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\|g\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s^{\prime}}_{{\boldsymbol{q}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}},

where the last series converges due to
s^{\prime}>s
. Next we show

\|fg\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{-s}_{1;{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\lesssim_{C}\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{-s}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\|g\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s^{\prime}}_{{\boldsymbol{q}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}.

By Bony’s decomposition (
2.7
), it suffices to show the following three estimates:

\displaystyle\|f\prec g\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s^{\prime}-s}_{1;{\boldsymbol{a}}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{C}\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{-s}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\|g\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s^{\prime}}_{{\boldsymbol{q}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}},

(2.15)

\displaystyle\|g\prec f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{-s}_{1;{\boldsymbol{a}}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{C}\|g\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{q}}}\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{-s}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}},

(2.16)

\displaystyle\|f\circ g\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{0}_{1;{\boldsymbol{a}}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{C}\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{-s}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\|g\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s^{\prime}}_{{\boldsymbol{q}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}.

(2.17)

Noting that (see
[
13
, (2.9)]
),

{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}(S_{k-1}f{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{k}g)=0,\ \ |k-j|>2,

by definition (
2.6
), (
2.10
) and Hölder’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}(f\prec g)\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{|k-j|\leqslant 2}\|S_{k-1}f{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{k}g\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{1}}\leqslant\sum_{|k-j|\leqslant 2}\|S_{k-1}f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{k}g\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{q}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{|k-j|\leqslant 2}\sum_{i=0}^{k-2}2^{si}2^{-ks^{\prime}}\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{-s}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\|g\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s^{\prime}}_{{\boldsymbol{q}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\lesssim 2^{j(s-s^{\prime})}\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{-s}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\|g\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s^{\prime}}_{{\boldsymbol{q}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}},

which gives (
2.15
) by definition. Similarly,

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}(g\prec f)\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{1}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{|k-j|\leqslant 2}\|S_{k-1}g{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{k}f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{1}}\lesssim\sum_{|k-j|\leqslant 2}\|S_{k-1}g\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{q}}}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{k}f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{|k-j|\leqslant 2}\Big(\|g\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{q}}}2^{sk}\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{-s}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\Big)\lesssim 2^{sj}\|g\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{q}}}\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{-s}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}},

which yields (
2.16
).
Moreover, we also have

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{j}(f\circ g)\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\circ g\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{1}}\leqslant\sum_{|i-k|\leqslant 2}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{i}f{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{k}g\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{1}}\lesssim\sum_{|i-k|\leqslant 2}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{i}f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}}\|{\mathcal{R}}^{\boldsymbol{a}}_{k}g\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{q}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{|i-k|\leqslant 2}2^{si}2^{-ks^{\prime}}\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{-s}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\|g\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s^{\prime}}_{{\boldsymbol{q}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\lesssim\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{-s}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\|g\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s^{\prime}}_{{\boldsymbol{q}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}},

which gives (
2.17
).
The proof is complete.
∎

Lemma 2.6
.

For any
{\boldsymbol{p}}\in[1,\infty]^{N}
and
s\in(0,1)
, there is a constant
C=C({\boldsymbol{p}},{\boldsymbol{a}},s)>0
such that for any nonnegative
u
,

\|u\|^{2}_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s/2}_{2{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\lesssim\|u^{2}\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}.

Proof.

Since
|a-b|\leqslant\sqrt{|a^{2}-b^{2}|}
for
a,b\geqslant 0
, by (
2.8
), we have

\displaystyle\|u\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s/2}_{2{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}

\displaystyle\asymp\|u\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{2{\boldsymbol{p}}}}+\sup_{h}\|u(\cdot+h)-u(\cdot)\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{2{\boldsymbol{p}}}}/|h|^{s/2}_{\boldsymbol{a}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|u\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{2{\boldsymbol{p}}}}+\sup_{h}\|\sqrt{|u^{2}(\cdot+h)-u^{2}(\cdot)|}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{2{\boldsymbol{p}}}}/|h|^{s/2}_{\boldsymbol{a}}

\displaystyle=\|u^{2}\|^{1/2}_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}+\sup_{h}\|u^{2}(\cdot+h)-u^{2}(\cdot)\|^{1/2}_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}/|h|^{s/2}_{\boldsymbol{a}}\asymp\|u^{2}\|^{1/2}_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}.

The proof is complete.
∎

2.2.
De-Giorgi’s class

In this subsection we present a general criterion for the local bound of a function in De-Giorgi’s class.
Let
{\mathscr{I}}\subset(1,\infty)^{N}
be an open multi-index set and
Q:=(Q_{\tau})_{\tau\in[1,2]}

be a family of increasing bounded open sets in
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
with

\displaystyle Q_{\tau}\cap Q^{c}_{\sigma}=\emptyset\ \mbox{ for }\tau<\sigma\ \mbox{ and }\cap_{\sigma>\tau}Q_{\sigma}=\bar{Q}_{\tau}.

(2.18)

We introduce the following De-Giorgi class associated with
{\mathscr{I}}
and
Q
.

Definition 2.7
.

We call a function
u\in L^{1}(Q_{2})
being in the De-Giorgi class
{\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{G}}^{+}_{\mathscr{I}}(Q)

if there are
{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}\in{\mathscr{I}}
,
i=1,\cdots,m
,
1\leqslant j<m
and
\lambda,{\mathcal{A}}\geqslant 0
such that for any
{\boldsymbol{p}}\in{\mathscr{I}}
, there is a constant
C_{\boldsymbol{p}}>0
such that
for any
1\leqslant\tau<\sigma\leqslant 2
and
\kappa\geqslant 0
,

\displaystyle(\sigma-\tau)^{\lambda}\|{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\tau}}(u-\kappa)^{+}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}}\lesssim_{C_{\boldsymbol{p}}}\sum_{i=1}^{j}\|{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\sigma}}(u-\kappa)^{+}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}}+{\mathcal{A}}\sum_{i=j+1}^{m}\|{\mathbf{1}}_{\{u>\kappa\}\cap Q_{\sigma}}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}}.

(2.19)

The aim of this subsection is to give an upper bound estimate of
u\in{\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{G}}^{+}_{\mathscr{I}}(Q)
.
First of all, we recall the following two iteration lemmas (see
[
10
]
).

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
(a_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
be a sequence of nonnegative real numbers. Suppose that for some
C_{0},\lambda>1
and
\delta>0
,

a_{n+1}\leqslant C_{0}\lambda^{n}a_{n}^{1+\delta}.

Then under
a_{1}\leqslant(C_{0}\lambda^{(1+\delta)/\delta})^{-1/\delta}
, we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n}=0.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
h(\tau)\geqslant 0
be bounded in
[\tau_{1},\tau_{2}]
with
\tau_{1}\geqslant 0
. Suppose that for some
\alpha\geqslant 0
,
\theta\in(0,1)
and any

\tau_{1}\leqslant\tau<\tau^{\prime}\leqslant\tau_{2}
,

h(\tau)\leqslant\theta h(\tau^{\prime})+(\tau^{\prime}-\tau)^{-\alpha}A+B.

Then there is a constant
C=C(\alpha,\theta)>0
such that

h(\tau_{1})\lesssim_{C}(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})^{-\alpha}A+B.

Now we can show the following main result of this subsection (cf.
[
39
]
).

Theorem 2.10
.

Let
u\in{\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{G}}^{+}_{\mathscr{I}}(Q)
. For any
p>0
, there are constants
\gamma,C>0
only
depending on
p
,
{\rm Vol}(Q_{2})
and the parameters
{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i},\lambda,C_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}
in the definition of
{\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{G}}^{+}_{\mathscr{I}}(Q)
such that
for all
1\leqslant\tau<\sigma\leqslant 2
,

\displaystyle\|u^{+}{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\tau}}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\infty}}\lesssim_{C}(\sigma-\tau)^{-\gamma}\|u^{+}{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\sigma}}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{p}}+{\mathcal{A}}.

(2.20)

Proof.

Fix
1\leqslant\tau<\sigma\leqslant 2
and
\kappa>0
, which will be determined below. For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, define

\tau_{n}=\tau+(\sigma-\tau)2^{1-n},\ \ \kappa_{n}:=\kappa\left(1-2^{1-n}\right),

and

\ w_{n}:=(u-\kappa_{n})^{+}.

Clearly, by (
2.18
),

\kappa_{n}\uparrow\kappa,\ \tau_{n}\downarrow\tau,\ \ Q_{\tau_{n+1}}\subset Q_{\tau_{n}}\downarrow\bar{Q}_{\tau}.

Since
{\mathscr{I}}
is an open set, for any
{\boldsymbol{p}}\in{\mathscr{I}}
, there is a
{\boldsymbol{p}}^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{I}}
such that
{\boldsymbol{p}}^{\prime}>{\boldsymbol{p}}
.
Let
{\boldsymbol{q}}=(q_{1},\cdots,q_{N})\in[1,\infty)^{N}
be defined by

\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}^{\prime}}+\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{q}}}=\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}.

By Hölder’s inequality, there is a constant
C=C(Q_{2},{\boldsymbol{q}})>0
such that

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\tau_{n+1}}}w_{n+1}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\tau_{n+1}}}w_{n+1}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}^{\prime}}}\|{\mathbf{1}}_{\{w_{n+1}\not=0\}\cap Q_{\tau_{n+1}}}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\|{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\tau_{n+1}}}w_{n+1}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}^{\prime}}}\|{\mathbf{1}}_{\{w_{n+1}\not=0\}\cap Q_{\tau_{n}}}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{q}}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{C}\|{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\tau_{n+1}}}w_{n+1}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}^{\prime}}}\|{\mathbf{1}}_{\{w_{n+1}\not=0\}\cap Q_{\tau_{n}}}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{1}}^{1/\max_{i}q_{i}},

(2.21)

where the last step is due to
{\mathbf{1}}_{A}^{p}={\mathbf{1}}_{A}
for any
p>0
.
Thus, for
{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}\in{\mathscr{I}}
being as in Definition
2.7
, if we let

a_{n}:=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\|{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\tau_{n}}}w_{n}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}},

then by (
2.21
), there are
{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{I}}
and
C_{1},\delta>0
such that

\displaystyle a_{n+1}\lesssim_{C_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\|{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\tau_{n+1}}}w_{n+1}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}^{\prime}_{i}}}\|{\mathbf{1}}_{\{w_{n+1}\not=0\}\cap Q_{\tau_{n}}}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{1}}^{\delta}.

(2.22)

On the other hand, noting that

w_{n}|_{w_{n+1}\not=0}=(u-\kappa_{n+1}+\kappa_{n+1}-\kappa_{n})^{+}|_{w_{n+1}\not=0}\geqslant\kappa_{n+1}-\kappa_{n}=\kappa 2^{-n},

for any
{\boldsymbol{p}}\in[1,\infty)^{N}
, we have

\displaystyle\|{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\tau_{n}}}w_{n}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}}\geqslant\|{\mathbf{1}}_{\{{w_{n+1}\not=0}\}\cap Q_{\tau_{n}}}w_{n}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}}\geqslant\kappa 2^{-n}\|{\mathbf{1}}_{\{w_{n+1}\not=0\}\cap Q_{\tau_{n}}}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}}.

(2.23)

Suppose

\kappa\geqslant{\mathcal{A}}.

For each
i=1,\cdots,m
, since
{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}^{\prime}\in{\mathscr{I}}
, by (
2.19
), (
2.23
) and
w_{n+1}\leqslant w_{n}
, we have

\displaystyle(\tau_{n}-\tau_{n+1})^{\lambda}\|{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\tau_{n+1}}}w_{n+1}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{i=1}^{j}\|{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\tau_{n}}}w_{n+1}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}}+{\mathcal{A}}\sum_{i=j+1}^{m}\|{\mathbf{1}}_{\{w_{n+1}>0\}\cap Q_{\tau_{n}}}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{i=1}^{j}\|{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\tau_{n}}}w_{n+1}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}}+2^{n}\sum_{i=j+1}^{m}\|{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\tau_{n}}}w_{n}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\|{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\tau_{n}}}w_{n}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}}=2^{n}a_{n}.

Substituting this into (
2.22
) and by
\tau_{n}-\tau_{n+1}=(\sigma-\tau)2^{-n}
and (
2.23
), we obtain that for
\kappa\geqslant{\mathcal{A}}
,

\displaystyle(\sigma-\tau)^{\lambda}2^{-\lambda n}a_{n+1}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{n}a_{n}\|{\mathbf{1}}_{\{w_{n+1}\not=0\}\cap Q_{\tau_{n}}}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{1}}^{\delta}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{n}a_{n}\|{\mathbf{1}}_{\{w_{n+1}\not=0\}\cap Q_{\tau_{n}}}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}}^{\delta}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{n}a_{n}(2^{n}\kappa^{-1})^{\delta}\|{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\tau_{n}}}w_{n}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}}^{\delta}

\displaystyle\leqslant 2^{n}a_{n}(2^{n}\kappa^{-1})^{\delta}a_{n}^{\delta}.

In particular, there is a
C_{2}>0
such that for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

a_{n+1}\leqslant C_{2}(\sigma-\tau)^{-\lambda}\kappa^{-\delta}2^{(\lambda+\delta+1)n}a_{n}^{1+\delta}.

Now let us choose

\kappa=\left(\left[C_{2}(\sigma-\tau)^{-\lambda}2^{(\lambda+\delta+1)(1+\delta)/\delta}\right]^{1/\delta}a_{1}\right)\vee{\mathcal{A}}.

Then by Fatou’s lemma and Lemma
2.8
,

\displaystyle\|(u-\kappa)^{+}{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\tau}}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}}\leqslant\liminf_{n\to\infty}\|w_{n}{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\tau_{n}}}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{1}}}\leqslant\limsup_{n\to\infty}a_{n}=0,

which in turn implies that
(u-\kappa)^{+}=0
on
Q_{\tau}
and by the choice of
\kappa
,

\displaystyle\|u^{+}{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\tau}}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leqslant\left(\left[C_{2}(\sigma-\tau)^{-\lambda}2^{(\lambda+\delta+1)(1+\delta)/\delta}\right]^{1/\delta}a_{1}\right)\vee{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{3}(\sigma-\tau)^{-\frac{\lambda}{\delta}}\sum_{i=0}^{m}\|u^{+}{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\sigma}}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}}+{\mathcal{A}}.

(2.24)

To show (
2.20
), without loss of generality, we may assume

p\leqslant\gamma/2,\ \ \gamma:=\max_{i,j}p_{ij},\ {\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}=(p_{i1},\cdots,p_{iN}).

By (
2.24
), Hölder’s inequality and Young’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\|u^{+}{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\tau}}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leqslant C(\sigma-\tau)^{-\frac{\lambda}{\delta}}\|u^{+}{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\sigma}}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\gamma}}+{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle\leqslant C(\sigma-\tau)^{-\frac{\lambda}{\delta}}\|u^{+}{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\sigma}}\|^{1-\frac{p}{\gamma}}_{\infty}\|u^{+}{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\sigma}}\|^{\frac{p}{\gamma}}_{{\mathbb{L}}^{p}}+{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\tfrac{1}{2}\|u^{+}{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\sigma}}\|_{\infty}+C(\sigma-\tau)^{-\frac{\gamma}{p\delta}}\|u^{+}{\mathbf{1}}_{Q_{\sigma}}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{p}}+{\mathcal{A}},

where
C
is independent of
\sigma,\tau
.
Thus by Lemma
2.9
, we obtain (
2.20
).
∎

3.
Weak (sub)-solutions of kinetic equations

In this section we present some basic properties about weak (sub)-solutions of linear kinetic FPKEs
and show the gain of regularity in
x
via Duhamel’s formula.
Consider the following kinetic equation of divergence form:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u=\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}(a\cdot\nabla_{v}u)+v\cdot\nabla_{x}u+b\cdot\nabla_{v}u+f,

(3.1)

where

a:{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}\to{\mathbb{M}}^{d}_{\rm sym},\ b:{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d},\ f:{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}\to{\mathbb{R}}

are Borel measurable functions.
Suppose that for some
0<\kappa_{0}<\kappa_{1}
,

\displaystyle\kappa_{0}{\mathbb{I}}\leqslant a\leqslant\kappa_{1}{\mathbb{I}},

(3.2)

and for any bounded
Q\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}
,

\displaystyle b{\mathbf{1}}_{Q}\in{\mathbb{L}}^{2},\ \ f{\mathbf{1}}_{Q}\in{\mathbb{L}}^{1},

(3.3)

where for
p,q\in[1,\infty]
,

{\mathbb{L}}^{q}_{t}({\mathbb{L}}^{p}_{z}):=L^{q}({\mathbb{R}};L^{p}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})),\ \ {\mathbb{L}}^{p}:={\mathbb{L}}^{p}_{t}({\mathbb{L}}^{p}_{z}).

We introduce the following space of solutions: for an open set
Q\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}
,

{\mathscr{V}}_{Q}:=\Big\{f\in{\mathbb{L}}^{1}_{loc}:\|f\|_{{\mathscr{V}}_{Q}}:=\|{\mathbf{1}}_{Q}f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\infty}_{t}({\mathbb{L}}^{2}_{z})}+\|{\mathbf{1}}_{Q}\nabla_{v}f\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{2}}<\infty\Big\}.

For simplicity, we write for any
T>0
,

{\mathscr{V}}_{T}:={\mathscr{V}}_{[0,T]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}},\ \ {\mathscr{V}}:={\mathscr{V}}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}},\ \ {\mathscr{V}}_{loc}:=\cap_{{\rm bounded}\,Q}{\mathscr{V}}_{Q},

and for given
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
r>0
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{I}}_{t}:={\mathbf{1}}_{(-\infty,t]},\ \ Q_{r}:=\big\{(t,x,v):|t|<r^{2},|x|<r^{3},|v|<r\big\}.

(3.4)

3.1.
Weak (sub)-solutions

In this subsection we introduce the notion of weak (sub)-solutions and their basic properties for later use.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
Q\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}
be a bounded domain.
A function
u\in{\mathscr{V}}_{Q}\cap{\mathbb{L}}^{\infty}_{Q}
is called a weak sub-solution of PDE (
3.1
) in
Q

if for any nonnegative
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(Q)
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}-\int_{Q}u\partial_{t}\varphi\leqslant&-\int_{Q}\langle a\cdot\nabla_{v}u,\nabla_{v}\varphi\rangle-\int_{Q}u(v\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi)+\int_{Q}(b\cdot\nabla_{v}u)\varphi+\int_{Q}f\varphi.\end{split}

(3.5)

If both
u
and
-u
are weak sub-solutions, then we call
u
a weak solution of PDE (
3.1
) in
Q
.
If
u\in{\mathscr{V}}_{loc}\cap{\mathbb{L}}^{\infty}_{loc}
and (
3.5
) holds for any bounded domain
Q
, then it is called a global weak solution.

Remark 3.2
.

By (
3.2
) and (
3.3
), each term in (
3.5
) is well-defined.
Let
u
be a global weak solution.
By a standard approximation, one sees that (
3.5
) is equivalent that for any
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{2d})
and Lebesgue almost all
t_{0}<t_{1}
,

\displaystyle\langle\!\!\!\langle u,\varphi\rangle\!\!\!\rangle|^{t_{1}}_{t_{0}}\leqslant

\displaystyle-\int^{t_{1}}_{t_{0}}\langle\!\!\!\langle a\cdot\nabla_{v}u,\nabla_{v}\varphi\rangle\!\!\!\rangle-\int^{t_{1}}_{t_{0}}\langle\!\!\!\langle v\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi,u\rangle\!\!\!\rangle+\int^{t_{1}}_{t_{0}}\langle\!\!\!\langle b\cdot\nabla_{v}u,\varphi\rangle\!\!\!\rangle+\int^{t_{1}}_{t_{0}}\langle\!\!\!\langle f,\varphi\rangle\!\!\!\rangle,

where
\langle\!\!\!\langle u,\varphi\rangle\!\!\!\rangle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}u(z)\varphi(z){\mathord{{\rm d}}}z
.
In particular, let
u
be a weak solution of the Cauchy problem of (
3.1
) with initial value
u(0)=0
. Then
it can be extended to be a global weak solution by
setting
u(t)=f(t)\equiv 0
for
t\leqslant 0
.

The following two lemmas are well known to experts. For readers’ convenience, we provide detailed proofs here.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
u\in{\mathscr{V}}_{Q}\cap{\mathbb{L}}^{\infty}_{Q}
be a nonnegative weak sub-solution of PDE (
3.1
) in
Q
. Under (
3.2
) and (
3.3
),
for any nonnegative
\eta\in C^{\infty}_{c}(Q)
and
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}|(u\eta)(t)|^{2}&\leqslant\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}u^{2}\eta(\partial_{s}\eta-v\cdot\nabla_{x}\eta){\mathcal{I}}_{t}-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}\langle a\cdot\nabla_{v}u,\nabla_{v}(u\eta^{2})\rangle{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\\
&\quad+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}(b\cdot\nabla_{v}u)u\eta^{2}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}fu\eta^{2}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}.\end{split}

(3.6)

Moreover, if
u
is a weak solution, then the above inequality becomes an equality.

Proof.

Let
\varGamma
be a nonnegative symmetric smooth function in
{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}
with compact support.
For
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, we introduce the following mollifier:

\displaystyle\varGamma_{\varepsilon}(t,x,v):=\varepsilon^{-(4d+2)}\varGamma\big(\tfrac{t}{\varepsilon^{2}},\tfrac{x}{\varepsilon},\tfrac{v}{\varepsilon^{3}}\big),\ \ u_{\varepsilon}:=u*\varGamma_{\varepsilon}.

(3.7)

Let
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(Q)
be nonnegative.
By the integration by parts, we have

-\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}u^{2}_{\varepsilon}\partial_{t}\phi=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}u_{\varepsilon}\phi=-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}u_{\varepsilon}\partial_{t}(u_{\varepsilon}\phi)=-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}u\partial_{t}(\varGamma_{\varepsilon}*(u_{\varepsilon}\phi)).

Thus by (
3.5
) with
\varphi=\varGamma_{\varepsilon}*(u_{\varepsilon}\phi)\in C^{\infty}_{c}(Q)
provided
\varepsilon
small enough, we get

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}u^{2}_{\varepsilon}\partial_{t}\phi

\displaystyle\leqslant-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}\langle a\cdot\nabla_{v}u,\varGamma_{\varepsilon}*\nabla_{v}(u_{\varepsilon}\phi)\rangle-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}u(v\cdot\varGamma_{\varepsilon}*\nabla_{x}(u_{\varepsilon}\phi))

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}(\varGamma_{\varepsilon}*(b\cdot\nabla_{v}u))u_{\varepsilon}\phi+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}(\varGamma_{\varepsilon}*f)u_{\varepsilon}\phi.

(3.8)

Fix
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Let
\chi_{n}
be a family of smooth functions in
{\mathbb{R}}
so that

\chi_{n}\to{\mathcal{I}}_{t}=1_{(-\infty,t]},\ \ \chi^{\prime}_{n}\to-\delta_{\{t\}}\ \ {\rm as}\ n\to\infty,

where
\delta_{\{t\}}
stands for the Dirac measure at point
t
.
Using
\chi_{n}\eta^{2}
in place of
\phi
in (
3.8
) and then taking limits
n\to\infty
, we obtain

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}|(u_{\varepsilon}\eta)(t)|^{2}-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}u^{2}_{\varepsilon}\partial_{s}\eta^{2}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\leqslant-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}\langle a\cdot\nabla_{v}u,\varGamma_{\varepsilon}*\nabla_{v}(u_{\varepsilon}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\eta^{2})\rangle-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}u(v\cdot\varGamma_{\varepsilon}*\nabla_{x}(u_{\varepsilon}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\eta^{2}))

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}(\varGamma_{\varepsilon}*(b\cdot\nabla_{v}u))u_{\varepsilon}\eta^{2}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}(\varGamma_{\varepsilon}*f)u_{\varepsilon}\eta^{2}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}.

(3.9)

By the definition of convolutions, we have

\displaystyle\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}u(v\cdot\nabla_{x}\varGamma_{\varepsilon}*(u_{\varepsilon}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\eta^{2}))-v\cdot\nabla_{x}(u*\varGamma_{\varepsilon})(u_{\varepsilon}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\eta^{2})\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}u(\bar{s},\bar{x},\bar{v})(\bar{v}-v)\cdot\nabla_{x}\varGamma_{\varepsilon}(s-\bar{s},x-\bar{x},v-\bar{v})\right)(u_{\varepsilon}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\eta^{2})(s,x,v)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}|u(\bar{s},\bar{x},\bar{v})|\,|\nabla_{x}\varGamma|\big(\tfrac{s-\bar{s}}{\varepsilon^{2}},\tfrac{x-\bar{x}}{\varepsilon},\tfrac{v-\bar{v}}{\varepsilon^{3}}\big)\right)|(u_{\varepsilon}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\eta^{2})(s,x,v)|,

which converges to zero as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Note that

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}(v\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{\varepsilon})(u_{\varepsilon}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\eta^{2})=\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}(v\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{\varepsilon}^{2})\eta^{2}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}(v\cdot\nabla_{x}\eta^{2})u_{\varepsilon}^{2}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}.

Substituting these into (
3.9
) and letting
\varepsilon\to 0
, we get

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}|(u\eta)(t)|^{2}\leqslant\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}u^{2}\eta\partial_{s}\eta{\mathcal{I}}_{t}-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}\langle a\cdot\nabla_{v}u,\nabla_{v}(u\eta^{2})\rangle{\mathcal{I}}_{t}

\displaystyle\qquad-\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}(v\cdot\nabla_{x}\eta^{2})u^{2}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}(b\cdot\nabla_{v}u)u\eta^{2}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}fu\eta^{2}{\mathcal{I}}_{t},

where
u\in{\mathbb{L}}^{\infty}_{Q}
is used for taking limits for

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}(\varGamma_{\varepsilon}*(b\cdot\nabla_{v}u))u_{\varepsilon}\eta^{2}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}(b\cdot\nabla_{v}u)u\eta^{2}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}

and

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}(\varGamma_{\varepsilon}*f)u_{\varepsilon}\eta^{2}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}fu\eta^{2}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}.

Thus we obtain (
3.6
). If
u
is a weak solution, then from the above proof, one sees that (
3.6
) takes equality.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

If
b=0
and
{\mathbf{1}}_{Q}f\in{\mathbb{L}}^{1}_{t}({\mathbb{L}}^{2}_{z})
, it suffices to require
u\in{\mathscr{V}}_{Q}
in Lemma
3.3
.

The second part of the following lemma shall be used to deal with the distribution-valued inhomogeneous
f
.

Lemma 3.5
.

(i)

Suppose that
u\in{\mathscr{V}}_{loc}\cap{\mathbb{L}}^{\infty}_{loc}
is a weak sub-solution of (
3.1
). Then
u^{+}
is still a weak sub-solution of (
3.1
) with

f{\mathbf{1}}_{\{u>0\}}
in place of
f
.

(ii)

Suppose that
u\in{\mathscr{V}}_{loc}\cap{\mathbb{L}}^{\infty}_{loc}
is a weak solution of (
3.1
). Then
(u^{+})^{2}
is still a weak solution of (
3.1
) with

2fu^{+}-2\langle a\cdot\nabla_{v}u^{+},\nabla_{v}u^{+}\rangle
in place of
f
.

Proof.

Let
\varGamma_{\varepsilon}
be as in (
3.7
). By taking
\varphi=\varGamma_{\varepsilon}(t-\cdot,x-\cdot,v-\cdot)
in (
3.5
), we obtain

\partial_{t}u_{\varepsilon}\leqslant\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}(a\cdot\nabla_{v}u)*\varGamma_{\varepsilon}+(v\cdot\nabla_{x}u+b\cdot\nabla_{v}u)*\varGamma_{\varepsilon}+f_{\varepsilon}.

First of all, we assume that
\beta:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is a smooth non-decreasing function and
u
is a weak sub-solution.
By the chain rule and
\beta^{\prime}\geqslant 0
, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\beta(u_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\leqslant(\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}(a\cdot\nabla_{v}u)*\varGamma_{\varepsilon}+(v\cdot\nabla_{x}u+b\cdot\nabla_{v}u)*\varGamma_{\varepsilon}+f_{\varepsilon})\beta^{\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=(\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}(a\cdot\nabla_{v}u_{\varepsilon})+v\cdot\nabla_{x}u_{\varepsilon}+b\cdot\nabla_{v}u_{\varepsilon}+H_{\varepsilon}+f_{\varepsilon})\beta^{\prime}(u_{\varepsilon}),

where

H_{\varepsilon}:=\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}(a\cdot\nabla_{v}u)*\varGamma_{\varepsilon}-\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}(a\cdot\nabla_{v}u_{\varepsilon})+[\varGamma_{\varepsilon}*,v\cdot\nabla_{x}]u+[\varGamma_{\varepsilon}*,b\cdot\nabla_{v}]u,

and

[\varGamma_{\varepsilon}*,v\cdot\nabla_{x}]u:=\varGamma_{\varepsilon}*(v\cdot\nabla_{x}u)-v\cdot\nabla_{x}(\varGamma_{\varepsilon}*u).

For any
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d})
, by the chain rule, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}-\int\beta(u_{\varepsilon})\partial_{t}\varphi&\leqslant-\int\langle a\cdot\nabla_{v}u_{\varepsilon},\nabla_{v}(\beta^{\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})\varphi)\rangle-\int(v\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi)\beta(u_{\varepsilon})\\
&\quad+\int b\cdot\nabla_{v}\beta(u_{\varepsilon})\varphi+\int(H_{\varepsilon}+f_{\varepsilon})\beta^{\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})\varphi.\end{split}

(3.10)

Note that
u
is locally bounded and

\langle a\cdot\nabla_{v}u_{\varepsilon},\nabla_{v}(\beta^{\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})\varphi)\rangle=\langle a\cdot\nabla_{v}\beta(u_{\varepsilon}),\nabla_{v}\varphi\rangle+\langle a\cdot\nabla_{v}u_{\varepsilon},\nabla_{v}u_{\varepsilon}\rangle\beta^{\prime\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})\varphi.

By easy calculations, we have

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int H_{\varepsilon}\beta^{\prime}(u_{\varepsilon})\varphi=0,

and by taking limits
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
for (
3.10
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}-\int\beta(u)\partial_{t}\varphi&\leqslant-\int\langle a\cdot\nabla_{v}\beta(u),\nabla_{v}\varphi\rangle-\int\langle a\cdot\nabla_{v}u,\nabla_{v}u\rangle\beta^{\prime\prime}(u)\varphi\\
&\quad-\int(v\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi)\beta(u)+\int b\cdot\nabla_{v}\beta(u)\varphi+\int f\beta^{\prime}(u)\varphi.\end{split}

(3.11)

(i) For
\varepsilon>0
, define

\beta_{\varepsilon}(r):=(\sqrt{(r-\varepsilon)^{2}+\varepsilon^{3}}+r-\varepsilon)/2.

It is easy to see that
\beta_{\varepsilon}\geqslant 0
is a smooth non-decreasing function and

\beta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}(r)\geqslant 0,\ \ \lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\beta_{\varepsilon}(r)=r^{+},\ \ \lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\beta^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}(r)={\mathbf{1}}_{\{r>0\}},\ \ r\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Using
\beta_{\varepsilon}
in place of the
\beta
in (
3.11
) and taking limits
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, by the dominated convergence theorem, one sees that

\displaystyle-\int u^{+}\partial_{t}\varphi

\displaystyle\leqslant-\int\langle a\cdot\nabla_{v}u^{+},\nabla_{v}\varphi\rangle-\int(v\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi)u^{+}+\int b\cdot\nabla_{v}u^{+}\varphi+\int f{\mathbf{1}}_{\{u>0\}}\varphi.

(ii) Note that

\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}(\beta^{2}_{\varepsilon}(r))^{\prime}=2r^{+},\ \ \lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}(\beta^{2}_{\varepsilon}(r))^{\prime\prime}=2{\mathbf{1}}_{r>0}.

If
u
is a weak solution, then (
3.11
) becomes an equality.
Using
\beta^{2}_{\varepsilon}
in place of the
\beta
in (
3.11
) and taking limits
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, one sees that

\displaystyle-\int(u^{+})^{2}\partial_{t}\varphi

\displaystyle=-\int\langle a\cdot\nabla_{v}(u^{+})^{2},\nabla_{v}\varphi\rangle-2\int\langle a\cdot\nabla_{v}u^{+},\nabla_{v}u^{+}\rangle\varphi

\displaystyle\quad-\int(v\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi)(u^{+})^{2}+\int b\cdot\nabla_{v}(u^{+})^{2}\varphi+2\int fu^{+}\varphi.

The proof is complete.
∎

3.2.
Gain of regularity via Duhamel’s formula

Below we take

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{a}}=\Big(\underbrace{3,\cdots,3}_{d},\underbrace{1,\cdots,1}_{d}\Big)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2d},\ \ |{\boldsymbol{a}}|=4d.

(3.12)

Consider the following simple model equation:

\partial_{t}u=\Delta_{v}u+v\cdot\nabla_{x}u+f,\ \ u|_{t\leqslant 0}=0.

By Duhamel’s formula, it’s unique solution can be represented by

\displaystyle u(t,z)=\int^{t}_{0}P_{t-s}f(s,z){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,

(3.13)

where
P_{t}
is the heat semigroup of operator
\Delta_{v}+v\cdot\nabla_{x}
given by

\displaystyle P_{t}f(z)={\mathbf{E}}f\left(x+tv+\sqrt{2}\int^{t}_{0}W_{s}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s,v+\sqrt{2}W_{t}\right),\ \ z=(x,v).

(3.14)

The following estimate is important for improving the regularity of
u
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
1\leqslant q\leqslant\nu\leqslant\infty
,
\gamma\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
{\boldsymbol{p}}\in[1,\infty]^{2d}
. For any
T>0
and
\beta<\gamma+2(1+\frac{1}{\nu}-\frac{1}{q})
,
there is a constant
C=C(T,d,{\boldsymbol{p}},q,\nu,\gamma,\beta)>0
such that

\displaystyle\|u{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\nu}_{t}({\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}})}\lesssim_{C}\|f{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{q}_{t}({\mathbf{B}}^{\gamma}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}})},\ t\in(0,T],

(3.15)

where
u
is defined by (
3.13
).
Here we use the convention
f(t)|_{t\leqslant 0}=0
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume
\gamma\leqslant\beta
.
Since
\beta<\gamma+2(1+\frac{1}{\nu}-\frac{1}{q})
, one can choose

0<\kappa\in(1-\tfrac{q}{\nu},(1-\tfrac{\beta-\gamma}{2})q).

Noting that by
[
39
, Lemma 3.3]
,

\|P_{t}f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}\lesssim t^{-\frac{\beta-\gamma}{2}}\|f\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\gamma}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}},\ \ t\in(0,T],

by definition (
3.13
) and Hölder’s inequality with respect to
(t-s)^{\kappa-1}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s
, we have

\displaystyle\|u(t)\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int^{t}_{0}(t-s)^{-\frac{\beta-\gamma}{2}}\|f(s)\|_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\gamma}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\int^{t}_{0}(t-s)^{\kappa-1}\|f(s)\|^{q}_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\gamma}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\right)^{1/q}.

Thus by Minkowskii’s inequality, for any
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\left(\int^{t}_{0}\|u(s)\|^{\nu}_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\right)^{1/\nu}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\int^{t}_{0}\left(\int^{s}_{0}(s-r)^{\kappa-1}\|f(r)\|^{q}_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\gamma}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}r\right)^{\nu/q}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\right)^{1/\nu}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\int^{t}_{0}\left(\int^{t}_{r}(s-r)^{\nu(\kappa-1)/q}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s\right)^{q/\nu}\|f(r)\|^{q}_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\gamma}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}r\right)^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leqslant C_{q,\kappa}{t}^{\frac{1}{\nu}+\frac{\kappa-1}{q}}\left(\int^{t}_{0}\|f(r)\|^{q}_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\gamma}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};{\boldsymbol{a}}}}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}r\right)^{1/q},

which gives (
3.15
). The proof is complete.
∎

We also need the following simple interpolation lemma.

Lemma 3.7
.

For any
\beta>0
,
1\leqslant q\leqslant\nu\leqslant\infty
and
{\boldsymbol{r}}\in[2,\infty]^{2d}
with

\displaystyle\tfrac{2}{{\boldsymbol{r}}}\geqslant(1-\tfrac{q}{\nu}){\mathbf{1}},\ \ {\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{r}}}-\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{2}}})+\tfrac{q\beta}{\nu}>0,

(3.16)

there is a constant
C=C(\beta,\nu,q,{\boldsymbol{r}},{\boldsymbol{a}})>0
such that

\|u\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\nu}_{t}({\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{r}}_{z})}\lesssim_{C}\|u\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\infty}_{t}({\mathbb{L}}^{2}_{z})}+\|u\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{q}_{t}({\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{{\boldsymbol{2}};a})}.

Proof.

Let
\theta:=\frac{q}{\nu}\in[0,1]
. Since
\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{r}}}\geqslant\tfrac{1-\theta}{{\boldsymbol{2}}}
, one can choose
{\boldsymbol{p}}\in[2,\infty]^{2d}
so that

\tfrac{\theta}{{\boldsymbol{p}}}+\tfrac{1-\theta}{{\boldsymbol{2}}}=\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{r}}}.

By Hölder’s inequality and Sobolev’s embedding (
2.12
), we have

\displaystyle\|u\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{r}}_{z}}\leqslant\|u\|^{1-\theta}_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{2}}_{z}}\|u\|^{\theta}_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{p}}_{z}}\lesssim\|u\|^{1-\theta}_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{2}}_{z}}\|u\|^{\theta}_{{\mathbf{B}}^{s}_{{\boldsymbol{p}};a}}\lesssim\|u\|^{1-\theta}_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{2}}_{z}}\|u\|^{\theta}_{{\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{{\boldsymbol{2}};a}},

where the second inequality is due to
s=\beta-{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{2}}}-\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}})>0
by the assumption. Hence,

\displaystyle\|u\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\nu}_{t}({\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{r}}_{z})}\lesssim\|u\|^{1-\theta}_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\infty}_{t}({\mathbb{L}}^{\boldsymbol{2}}_{z})}\|u\|^{\theta}_{{\mathbb{L}}^{q}_{t}({\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{{\boldsymbol{2}};a})}.

The proof is complete.
∎

Remark 3.8
.

The first condition in (
3.16
) means that for fixed
q
,
if
\nu
goes to infinity, then
{\boldsymbol{r}}
has to be close to
{\boldsymbol{2}}
whatever
\beta
is how large and the second condition holds.
However, for
\beta\in(0,\frac{1}{2}]
,

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{r}}}-\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{2}}})+\tfrac{q\beta}{\nu}>0\Rightarrow\tfrac{2}{{\boldsymbol{r}}}\geqslant(1-\tfrac{q}{\nu}){\mathbf{1}}.

(3.17)

Indeed, for
{\boldsymbol{r}}=(r_{1},\cdots,r_{2d})\in[2,\infty]^{2d}
, and for each
i
, we have

\displaystyle 0<{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{r}}}-\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{2}}})+\tfrac{q\beta}{\nu}\leqslant a_{i}\cdot(\tfrac{1}{r_{i}}-\tfrac{1}{2})+\tfrac{q}{2\nu}\leqslant\tfrac{1}{r_{i}}-\tfrac{1}{2}+\tfrac{q}{2\nu}.

For any
T>0
,
q\in[1,\infty]
and a Banach space
{\mathbb{B}}
, we denote

{\mathbb{L}}^{q}_{T}({\mathbb{B}}):=L^{q}([0,T];{\mathbb{B}}).

Now we consider the following Cauchy problem of the kinetic equation

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u=\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}(a\cdot\nabla_{v}u)+v\cdot\nabla_{x}u+f,\ \ u|_{t\leqslant 0}\equiv 0,

(3.18)

where the inhomogeneous
f
satisfies that for any
T>0
,

(H
f
)

f=\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}F+f_{1}+\cdots+f_{m}
, where
F\in{\mathbb{L}}^{2}_{T}({\mathbb{L}}^{2}_{z})
and for each
i=1,\cdots,m
,

f_{i}\in{\mathbb{L}}^{q_{i}}_{T}({\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}_{z})

for some
(q_{i},{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i})\in[1,2]^{1+2d}
with

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}\leqslant(\tfrac{3}{2}-\tfrac{1}{q_{i}}){\mathbf{1}},\ \ \ {\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}-\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{2}}})+\tfrac{2}{q_{i}}<2.

(3.19)

We have the following solvability result for equation (
3.18
).

Theorem 3.9
.

Under (
3.2
) and
(H
f
)
, there is a unique weak solution
u
to PDE (
3.18
)
so that for any
T>0
and
\beta\in(0,1)
, there is a constant
C=C(T,{\boldsymbol{a}},\kappa_{i},q_{i},{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i},\beta)>0
such that

\displaystyle\|u{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathscr{V}}}+\|u{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{2}_{t}({\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{2;{\boldsymbol{a}}})}\lesssim_{C}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\|f_{i}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{q_{i}}_{t}({\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}_{z})}+\|F{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{2}},\ \ t\in[0,T].

(3.20)

Here we use the convention
f_{i}(t)=F(t)=0
for
t\leqslant 0
.

Proof.

We only prove the apriori estimate (
3.20
). The existence follows by the standard continuity method.
Let
\chi\in C^{\infty}_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d})
with
\chi=1
on
Q_{1}
and
\chi=0
on
Q^{c}_{2}
. For
R\geqslant 1
,
let

\displaystyle\chi_{R}(t,x,v):=\chi(t/R^{2},x/R^{3},v/R).

(3.21)

By taking
\eta=\chi_{R}
in Lemma
3.3
, we have for any
t\in(0,T]
,

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}|(u\chi_{R})(t)|^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}u^{2}\chi_{R}(\partial_{s}\chi_{R}-v\cdot\nabla_{x}\chi_{R}){\mathcal{I}}_{t}

\displaystyle-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}\langle a\cdot\nabla_{v}u,\nabla_{v}(u\chi_{R}^{2})\rangle{\mathcal{I}}_{t}+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}fu\chi_{R}^{2}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}.

Noting that for some
C>0
independent of
R>0
,

\|\partial_{s}\chi_{R}-v\cdot\nabla_{x}\chi_{R}\|_{\infty}\leqslant C/R^{2},

by the dominated convergence theorem and letting
R\to\infty
, we obtain

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2d}}|u(t)|^{2}

\displaystyle=-\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}\langle a\cdot\nabla_{v}u,\nabla_{v}u\rangle{\mathcal{I}}_{t}-\int_{{\mathcal{I}}^{t}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}F\cdot\nabla_{v}u{\mathcal{I}}_{t}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}f_{i}u{\mathcal{I}}_{t}.

By the uniform ellipticity (
3.2
) of
a
, we have

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}\langle a\cdot\nabla_{v}u,\nabla_{v}u\rangle{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\geqslant\kappa_{0}\|\nabla_{v}u{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|^{2}_{{\mathbb{L}}^{2}}.

By Hölder’s inequality and Young’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}F\cdot\nabla_{v}u{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\right|\leqslant\|F{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{2}}\|\nabla_{v}u{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{2}}\leqslant\tfrac{\kappa_{0}}{4}\|\nabla_{v}u{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{2}}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{\kappa_{0}}\|F{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|^{2}_{{\mathbb{L}}^{2}},

and for
\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i}}+\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}=1
,
\frac{1}{\nu_{i}}+\frac{1}{q_{i}}=1
and any
\varepsilon>0
,

\left|\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{1+2d}}f_{i}u{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\right|\leqslant\|u{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\nu_{i}}_{t}({\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i}}_{z})}\|f_{i}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{q_{i}}_{t}({\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}_{z})}\leqslant\varepsilon\|u{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|^{2}_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\nu_{i}}_{t}({\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i}}_{z})}+\tfrac{1}{4\varepsilon}\|f_{i}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|^{2}_{{\mathbb{L}}^{q_{i}}_{t}({\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}_{z})}.

Combining the above calculations, we obtain that for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|u{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathscr{V}}}\lesssim_{C}\|F{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{2}}+\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{m}\|u{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\nu_{i}}_{t}({\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i}}_{z})}+\varepsilon^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\|f_{i}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{q_{i}}_{t}({\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}_{z})}.\end{split}

(3.22)

On the other hand, we may write

\partial_{t}u=\Delta_{v}u+v\cdot\nabla_{x}u+\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}((a-{\mathbb{I}})\cdot\nabla_{v}u+F)+\sum_{i=1}^{m}f_{i}.

By Duhamel’s formula, we have

\displaystyle u(t)

\displaystyle=\int^{t}_{0}P_{t-s}\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}((a-{\mathbb{I}})\cdot\nabla_{v}u+F){\mathord{{\rm d}}}s+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\int^{t}_{0}P_{t-s}f_{i}{\mathord{{\rm d}}}s=:I_{0}(t)+\sum_{i=1}^{m}I_{i}(t).

Below we fix
\beta\in(0,1)
and estimate each
I_{i}(t)
as following.

\bullet

For
I_{0}(t)
,
by (
3.15
) with
(\gamma,q,\nu,{\boldsymbol{p}})=(-1,2,2,{\boldsymbol{2}})
and Lemma
2.3
, we have

\displaystyle\|I_{0}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{2}_{t}({\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{2;{\boldsymbol{a}}})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\mathord{{\rm div}}_{v}((a-{\mathbb{I}})\cdot\nabla_{v}u+F){\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{2}_{t}({\mathbf{B}}^{-1}_{2;{\boldsymbol{a}}})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|((a-{\mathbb{I}})\cdot\nabla_{v}u+F){\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\kappa_{1}\|\nabla_{v}u{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{2}}+\|F{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{2}}.

\bullet

For
I_{i}(t)
, let
\beta_{i}>0
be defined by

\beta_{i}:=\beta+{\boldsymbol{a}}\cdot(\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}-\tfrac{1}{{\boldsymbol{2}}})<\beta+2-\tfrac{2}{q_{i}}<3-\tfrac{2}{q_{i}}.

By (
2.12
) and (
3.15
) with
(\gamma,\nu)=(0,2)
, we have for
i=1,\cdots,m
,

\displaystyle\|I_{i}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{2}_{t}({\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{2;{\boldsymbol{a}}})}\lesssim\|I_{i}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{2}_{t}({\mathbf{B}}^{\beta_{i}}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{a}}})}\lesssim\|f_{i}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{q_{i}}_{t}({\mathbf{B}}^{0}_{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i};{\boldsymbol{a}}})}\lesssim\|f_{i}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{q_{i}}_{t}({\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}_{z})}.

Combining the above calculations, we obtain

\displaystyle\|u{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{2}_{t}({\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{2;{\boldsymbol{a}}})}\lesssim\|\nabla_{v}u{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{2}}+\|F{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{2}}+\sum_{i=1}^{m}\|f_{i}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{q_{i}}_{t}({\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}_{z})},

which, together with (
3.22
),
yields that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
\beta\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|u{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathscr{V}}}+\|u{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{2}_{t}({\mathbf{B}}^{\beta}_{2;{\boldsymbol{a}}})}&\leqslant C\|F{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{2}}+\varepsilon\sum_{i=1}^{m}\|u{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{\nu_{i}}_{t}({\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i}}_{z})}+C_{\varepsilon}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\|f_{i}{\mathcal{I}}_{t}\|_{{\mathbb{L}}^{q_{i}}_{t}({\mathbb{L}}^{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}_{z})}.\end{split}

(3.23)

Recalling
\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i}}+\frac{1}{{\boldsymbol{p}}_{i}}=1
and
\frac{1}{\nu_{i}}+\frac{1}{q_{i}}=1
, by (
3.19
), we have
